## Document 301: How bad is the rise in bond yields_ (2026-09-23)

Source PDF: all report pdf/How bad is the rise in bond yields_ (2026-09-23).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-23
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1

SPECIAL EDITION | HOW BAS IS THE RISE 
IN BOND YIELDS?

TRANSCRIPTION

0:00:10 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Everyone, welcome. As we head into autumn 2026, interest rates are reaching levels never seen for many, many years. 
Why is this happening? Is it sustainable? And what are the implications for the economies of different countries? All these 
questions will be discussed over the next few minutes with the economists of BNP Paribas. Let us begin with Isabelle 
Mateos y Lago, chief economist of the group. Hello, Isabelle.  
0:00:38 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Hello Emmanuel, hello everyone. 
0:00:39 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you for being with us today. Just to begin, a simple explanation. What rates are we talking about and which countries 
are affected? 
0:00:48 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Yes, it is very important to clarify this. So, we are talking about long-term interest rates, rates at 10 years or longer 
maturities, 30 years, etc. The rates at which sovereign states borrow on the market. And this is primarily something that is 
happening in advanced countries, so United States, United King dom, Japan, and then the Eurozone countries. And indeed, 
as you said, we are seeing since the beginning of the summer, interest rates reach levels we had not seen for about 20 
years. But it is important to keep in mind that up until that point, up until the mid -2000s, the level of interest rates that 
we're seeing now were really quite common, quite normal, so one shouldn't overdramatize.  
0:01:35 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Why do we find ourselves in this situation and how did we get to this point?  
0:01:41 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Yes, so the direct cause is that investors are demanding higher interest rates in order to  lend their capital to governments. 
But why is this? Well, there's essentially three reasons for this, and not all of them are bad, and they're not mutually 
exclusive either. The top reason to my mind is that there's just greater competition for capital be tween sovereign states 
and the corporate sector, who are all facing simultaneously additional investment needs, in defense, in artificial 
intelligence, in energy transition, in resilience. And so,  you just have greater competition for capital and that pushes up the 
price of capital, which is the interest rate. 
Reason number two is because markets have revised upwards their expectations of how high central banks are going to be 
taking their interest rates. 
And this mechanically translates into higher rates at the long end. And then there's a third reason, which doesn't apply 
necessarily in every case, but which is essentially higher sovereign risk, a kind of a risk premium, in cases where there are  
concerns arising about the sustainability of these public debts.

### Page 2

2

0:02:59 - Emmanuel Laborde 
What are the implications for global economies? 
0:03:03 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
So that's exactly what we'll be discussing in the next segments. But basically, the two key issues are, one, an adverse 
impact on public finances. It's higher interest rates making it harder to balance the books in terms of public accounts. And 
then secondly, these higher rates tend to slow down economic growth. Of course, by how much and how serious it will be, 
depends on how high interest rates go and how long they stay there. But that's for the next segments.  
0:03:38 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Yes. Thank you very much, Isabelle. Having said the scene now, let's move on to the first segment with the following 
question. What makes debt sustainable? And let's see what Hélène Baudchon has to say.  
Hello Hélène and welcome into this special edition. 
0:03:58 - Hélène Baudchon 
Hello Emmanuel, hello everybody. 
0:04:01 - Emmanuel Laborde 
When economists talk about public debt, they often refer to the sustainability of debt. As an economist, can you explain to 
us what that means and what is the debt trajectory deemed unsustainable?  
0:04:15 - Hélène Baudchon 
To begin with, I will say that a temporary increase in debt can be seen as a normal evolution, not particularly concerning, 
for instance during an economic crisis or more recently during a health crisis. In such cases, it acts as a buffer. A problem  
arises when the debt level never comes back down. An unsustainable trajectory can be therefore defined, characterized by 
a continuous regular rise in the debt level, both in absolute terms and as a proportion of GDP, driven by high and recurring 
fiscal deficits. And sometimes interest payments can exacerbate the problem.  
0:05:00 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In very simple terms, you are talking about the snowball effect.  
0:05:03 - Hélène Baudchon 
Exactly explaining why consolidation measures are needed in order to prevent such an adverse situation from occurring 
and in order to prevent, in fact, a sudden loss of confidence from creditors.  
0:05:16 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Regarding governments, what should serve as a warning sign for them? And at what point could the situation become, let's 
say, problematic? 
0:05:27 - Hélène Baudchon 
Well, simply speaking, when interest rates rise, not so long ago, up until 2022, Eurozone countries were borrowing at very 
low rates and in some instance, even negative rates. And although debt levels were already at high level at that time, the 
low rates helped manage debt servicing. Not much fiscal effort was needed to keep the public debt ratios in check.  
0:05:55 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Of course, we all remember this because it was a very unusual situation, but now things are very different.

### Page 3

3

0:06:01 - Hélène Baudchon 
Yes, indeed, the situation today is clearly more challenging simply because bond yields are significantly higher and they 
are rising at the time of our discussion. This is for the part the result of the ECB's monetary tightening in 2022 -2023 aimed 
at addressing the inflation shock then. Then long-term interest rates have risen as a consequence and have aligned more 
closely with GDP growth rate. And in technical terms, this is what we refer to as a narrowing of the "r" - "g" spread. 
0:06:41 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Why is this spread so important? Why do you, the economists, monitor it this way?  
0:06:48 - Hélène Baudchon 
Essentially, you have the interest rate "r", which determines the debt burden, while the growth rate "g" determines the 
trajectory of fiscal revenues. And if "r" is lower than "g", and this has been the case for many years, as you can see on the  
chart on the screen, it means that interest payments are increasing at a slower pace than the revenue component of the 
equation. 
0:07:14 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And this provides some leeway in other budgetary areas, such as education, of course, defense, maybe pensions.  
0:07:22 - Hélène Baudchon 
Yes, indeed. And this is what we refer to as the primary budget. However, if and when interest rates do go up while the 
economy isn't growing that much faster, then fiscal leeway diminishes.  
0:07:35 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Crystal clear. Now, just could you provide a few last words to wrap up the sustainability segment?  
0:07:43 - Hélène Baudchon 
Well, to conclude, we are entering a period which demands greater fiscal discipline or, as some might say, less indiscipline 
in order to manage debt trajectories effectively, in particular in the countries which have high debt levels. And for instanc e, 
the Italian case is an interesting one because it is well known for its high debt level ratio. But in fact, it also has an 
advantageous position because it has no primary deficit. In fact, it even benefits from a large primary surplus.  
0:08:22 - Emmanuel Laborde 
As for the countries... For the other countries... I stop you because this will be a discussion with Guillaume Derrien during  
the next segment. Thank you very much, Hélène. 
0:08:30 - Hélène Baudchon 
Thank you, Emmanuel. 
0:08:36 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Hello, Guillaume. Welcome. 
0:08:37 - Guillaume Derrien 
Thank you. 
0:08:38 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Hello, Emmanuel, and hello to everyone. The flexibility of fiscal policy depends heavily on interest rates, which have 
increased considerably over the past few months. The question is, where do we stand today?

### Page 4

4

0:08:52 - Guillaume Derrien 
So, long-term rates have increased in all advanced economies, reaching levels not seen since 2008 in Europe and in the 
United States, and even since 1996 in Japan. Fortunately, the effective rate that states pay on their debt is lower than thos e 
headline figures suggest, simply because the average maturity of debt is relatively long. Precisely how long are we talking 
about? So, it's about 10 years in Japan, 13 years in the United Kingdom, and 8.5 years in France. That means that most of 
the debt was raised before rates started climbing. 
For example, in France, the effective rate just recently rose above the 2% mark, which is significantly lower than the 10 -
year bond yield, which exceeded 4% for new issuances.  
That said, in the United States, which has an average maturity of roughly six years, is experiencing a more rapid increase in  
its effective rate. 
0:09:54 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And this rate increase is happening in the context of high debt -to-GDP ratios and with modest growth, correct? Yes, that's 
correct. 
0:10:05 - Guillaume Derrien 
Debt levels have reached unprecedented heights since the end of World War II. And at the same time, growth is lower, 
hovering around 2% on average in the United States, 1% in Europe and 0.5% in Japan. What does that mean for debt 
servicing costs? So, debt servicing cost is already relatively high, but it is expected to grow further as debt issue before the 
2022 rate hikes mature and are replaced with new instruments bearing higher interest rates. By 2030, interest costs could 
rise to levels last seen in 2000, specifically in France, around 4% of GDP. 
Are all the countries equally affected by these trends? Actually, no. Some countries are managing to reduce their debt 
level. 
Spain, Italy, even Japan have sufficiently improved their primary balance to achieve this, at least for the time being. And o f 
course, we must speak about Germany. Yes, Germany is implementing investment programs that result in a higher public 
deficit and debt-to-GDP ratio. But this ratio started from a relatively low base at around above 60% of GDP. That said, in 
this current context, German bonds have risen. And because German bonds are used as a benchmark for Europe, it  raises 
rates across all Eurozone countries. Is France the only country seeing its public debt go up?  
No, it's not, Emmanuel. This is happening in the United Kingdom and in the United States. These economies, like France, 
are running public deficits that are ever close or exceed 5% of GDP, which is too large for debt ratio to stabilize on its ow n. 
France and the United Kingdom are trying to gradually reduce their public deficit so as not to compromise growth. The 
United States is not going on that path yet, but market pressure could eventually force them to do so.  
0:12:08 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much for your input, Guillaume. Thank you, Emmanuel. Let's go to the next segment. It's about the impact 
of rising bond yields on financing and investments in the Eurozone.  
0:12:26 - Thomas Humblot 
Hello, Thomas. Welcome. Hello. Thank you for having me today.  
0:12:30 - Emmanuel Laborde 
We've just seen what's behind the rise in Eurozone bond yields. This rise is partly attributed to the sovereign spread. What 
impact will this have on the economy? 
0:12:42 - Thomas Humblot 
Actually, this widening of some government bond spreads is obviously not good news, but it tends paradoxically to have 
less impact than an increase in long-term interest rates that does not include the sovereign risk premium. for example, in 
France, for a very long time, it has been considered that the 10 -year OAT was the relevant benchmark for fixed loans. And 
at that time, the spread on French government bonds was relatively stable. But since 2024, that spread increased

### Page 5

5

significantly and that renders the sovereign yield a less relevant benchmark.  
And now the most relevant benchmark is the swap rate because it determines the cost of bank funding that backs new 
loans. 
0:13:56 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Since the end of 2024, we have seen a rise in long-term 10-year swap rates. What impact does this have on bank lending 
rates? 
0:14:08 - Thomas Humblot 
Fixed loans have obviously become more expensive, but to a lesser extent than if they were priced solely based on long -
term sovereign rates. And what about existing fixed-rate loans? 
There is no impact. An increase in rates for the entire maturity of a loan does not have any effect if it is a fixed -rate loan. 
Borrowers with outstanding loans are therefore protected. Obviously, new fixed loans are granted at higher rates. And this 
sharply contrasts with a floating-rates loans. 
Because with floating-rates loans, borrowers experience regular rate adjustments throughout the term of the loan. And in 
terms of bank financing? Strictly in terms of bank financing, an increase in short -term rates has an immediate and 
widespread restrictive impact on borrowers, while for long-term interest rates, the impact is more gradual, and it comes 
gradually as the outstanding amount of fixed-rate loans are progressively renewed. And the thing is that since the 
beginning of the war in Iran, we have seen a combination of the two.  
And in practical terms, the rate of new bank loans to non-financial corporations has increased about 25 basis points in the 
eurozone since the beginning of the year. 
0:16:12 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Just a few words about companies that borrow directly from the market.  
0:16:16 - Thomas Humblot 
Well, in that case, the impact is even more straightforward. The rate at which a company borrows from the market is the 
risk-free rate plus a premium. 
0:16:28 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Have risk premiums, known as corporate credit spreads, also increased?  
0:16:34 - Thomas Humblot 
Yes, they have increased temporarily at the beginning of the year but have returned to normal since. For the time being, the 
widening remains moderate for the highest-rated investment-grade issuers. 
However, some significant bond issuance in the euro debt market due to some major US technology companies has 
increased spreads for longer maturities. 
0:17:11 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In particular terms, how does this affect the investment decisions of the companies?  
0:17:19 - Thomas Humblot 
Well, investments that were previously worthwhile due to returns sufficient to cover the cost of capital may no longer do so 
and in turn that might lead to a decrease in demand for financing.  
0:17:40 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much Thomas. Now let us move on to the emerging economies. And it is with Cynthia Kalasopatan -Antoine.

### Page 6

6

Hello Cynthia, and welcome. 
0:17:54 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Hi, Emmanuel. Hello, everyone. 
0:17:57 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Long-term bond yields rose significantly in advanced economies over the summer. What can we now say about emerging 
markets? 
0:18:06 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Emerging bond markets held up well, which is quite unusual. The rise in long -term sovereign yields in emerging countries 
has been less pronounced than in advanced economies, including the United States and Germany. Now, there are two 
main exceptions, Turkey and Poland. Meanwhile, we did not witness any significant correction in emerging financial 
markets over the summer, and the main currencies appreciated slightly against the dollar or the euro.  
0:18:37 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Does this mean that public finances are more robust in emerging countries?  
0:18:43 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, it depends. You see, in cases like the Czech Republic and South Korea, public debt is moderate and macroeconomic 
fundamentals are solid. However, these countries are now less frequently considered as emerging and tend to be viewed 
as newly advanced economies. By contrast, Brazil and South Africa have weaker public finances and investors have 
already incorporated a higher sovereign risk into their assessments. And this is reflected in the bond yields. And this 
assessment of sovereign risk did not really change over the summer. 
0:19:26 - Emmanuel Laborde 
When comparing the main fiscal metrics, what are the trends? What do you see as an economist?  
0:19:33 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
On average, the fiscal metrics in emerging markets are not substantially worse than those in advanced economies. The 
average budget deficit levels are similar, ranging from 4% to 5% of GDP in 2025 -2026, according to the IMF. And as in 
advanced economies, the public debt is higher than its pre-Covid level. However, the average public debt to GDP ratio in 
emerging countries excluding China is close to 60%, whereas in advanced economies, it is above 100%.  
0:20:12 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Do you arrive at the same conclusion when comparing debt sustainability criteria?  
0:20:17 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, there are significant regional differences. In emerging Asia, the factors that determine debt sustainability are more 
often favorable than those in advanced economies. In Central Europe, the gap between the interest rate paid on debt and 
the GDP growth rate is also favorable. However, the primary deficits in some countries are high.  
0:20:45 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In a few words, a brief overview of the situation in Latin America.  
0:20:49 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, Brazil and Mexico are in a less favorable position. These countries would indeed need to generate a significant 
primary budget surplus each year in order to stabilize their debt in the medium term.

### Page 7

7

0:21:07 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Economists use the term original sin, which refers to the situation when governments resort to borrowing in foreign 
currency to address the shortfall in domestic financing. Does this phenomenon continue to distinguish emerging 
countries? 
0:21:24 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Yes, this is still the case. Emerging countries still have a significant share of their debt in foreign currency. This repres ents 
about 35 to 40% of their total debt on average. And in fact, now, this figure has decreased over the past decade, and this 
contributes to their resilience. However, governments in emerging markets have a less favorable position as regards their 
debt profile and their financing costs are also on average higher.  
0:22:02 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And finally, just a few words about a larger subject, China.  
0:22:07 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
China's public debt is significant and growing rapidly. That being said, it is denominated in domestic currency and issued in  
domestic markets. This debt is, however, sustainable in the medium term due to a very low effective interest rate and a 
high growth rate. 
0:22:29 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much, Cynthia, for being with us today.  
0:22:32 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Thank you. 
0:22:33 - Emmanuel Laborde 
It is now time to conclude, and we welcome back Group Chief Economist Isabelle Mateos y Lago.  
It is time for our conclusion with Isabelle. Once again, just a final question, please. Could or should, I do not know which 
word is the good one, central banks intervene to curb this rise in interest rates?  
0:22:58 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Absolutely not. The main contribution that central banks can bring to dealing with this situation is to restore price stabili ty, 
as is their mandate, as promptly as possible. And in the current circumstances, this means keeping relatively high policy 
rates, in some cases, rising them further. And the responsibility for keeping public finances on a sustainable footing rests 
squarely with governments and any attempt by these governments to instead take the easy way out by pressuring central 
banks to lower interest rates prematurely would be enormously counterproductive.  
0:23:41 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much, Isabelle. Thank you all for joining us today. Just a few words to remind you that additional economic 
research content can be found by scanning the QR code shown on your screen. Do not hesitate to use it with your 
smartphone. It is very easy. It was a pleasure to be with you today. Have a nice day and hope to see you so

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
Markets have had little respite over the past month. Hopes 
of a lasting US–Iran agreement have faded and intermittent 
attacks on shipping have kept Brent oil prices in triple digits. 
Workarounds have allowed a meaningful share of crude to 
continue flowing but these arrangements are slower and 
costlier, while depleted inventories leave little room to 
absorb another shock and a range of disruptions to refining 
and shipping are also impacting downstream energy costs. 
Bond markets have been just as restless, with continued lifts 
in government bond yields across much of the globe and 
more sharply in the US. 
Notably, oil is no longer doing the heavy lifting for yields. 
The rise has been concentrated in real yields, with the Fed’s 
September hike – and its signal of more to come – acting as 
something of a trigger. In the US, fiscal concerns have been 
more of a sub-plot, though the Treasury’s efforts to hold 
down long yields have arguably only drawn more attention 
to them. 
In our view, markets are getting ahead of themselves on 
monetary policy offshore. We expect just one more hike 
from the Fed rather than the three the market currently has 
priced, with stretched housing affordability posing risks to 
both consumption and inflation. Central banks in Europe, the 
UK and Canada are also likely to bide their time after a final 
move. Europe is weathering the energy shock better than 
feared and has far more fiscal room to respond than the US – 
one reason we still see the US dollar trending lower over the 
next two years.
The RBA hiked again in September and looks set to follow 
up in November, taking the cash rate to a new cycle high. 
Some of the risks the Board flagged have crystallised, 
but the minutes also reveal a tendency to reach for the 
more hawkish read of the data, in addition to the familiar 
downbeat take on supply capacity. Our base case assumes 
no return to full-scale conflict, but with energy buffers 
depleted, bond markets less forgiving and the RBA already 
leaning hawkish, the margin for error – for markets and 
policymakers alike – is starting to ‘wear thin’.
Australia: After a soft but better than expected first half, the 
Australian economy is facing into more challenges as another 
round of disruptions from global energy markets combine with 
further interest rate rises and a deepening housing downturn. 
The demand mix should see capacity pressures continue to 
ease but with significant risks on either side.
Middle East: Low inventories and higher shipping costs will 
keep oil and fuel prices elevated, with Brent futures forecast 
to average US$101/bbl in Q4 before easing to US$77/bbl by 
Q4 2027 as supply recovers. Risks are two-sided: escalation 
could push Brent towards US$150/bbl, while an early US-Iran 
agreement could see a faster normalisation in supply.
Commodities: Most commodities rose in September, with 
many of the themes that drove moves across August remaining 
in place. Japanese LNG and Brent oil recorded strong gains 
amid Middle East tensions. Copper and aluminium extended 
recent gains while iron ore was supported by higher freight 
and production costs. Gold fell after the Fed’s September rate 
hike. 
Global FX Markets: In late September and early-October 
the DXY index popped higher to near 103, a high back to 
early-2025. Underlying the move are expectations that 
inflation will prove persistent globally as well as a belief that 
the US’ tech sector leaves it best positioned to weather the 
storm.
New Zealand: Higher fuel costs and a weaker exchange rate 
have lifted the short-term inflation outlook while the economy 
has continued to grow through the Iran war. Growth looks set 
to continue to be buoyed by strong commodity prices and a 
supportive exchange rate. However, downside risks are growing 
from higher energy costs and long-term interest rates. 
United States: The US housing market remains under 
considerable pressure. Prices continue to edge up, supporting 
wealth. But up-and-coming main street consumers are 
struggling with affordability and availability, posing enduring 
risks for both consumption growth and inflation.   
China: With each month that passes, the risk of disappointing 
authorities’ 4.5-5.0% ambition grows. While the Government 
is right to take a targeted approach to support, it must quickly 
multiply its scale if downside risks are to be kept at bay. 
Asia: For much of the past twenty years, Asia has been a 
source of disinflation for the world. But a combination of ageing 
populations, rising labour costs, increasing electricity demand 
and technology sector developments imply Asia may provide 
less support to global disinflation over the decade ahead.
Europe & UK: The euro has come under considerable pressure 
during the past month. That said, the European economy 
continues to tick along nicely while inflation risks remain 
manageable, allowing the ECB to be measured in their response.

Wearing thin

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FINANCIAL MARKETS
The main development affecting global markets this month
has been the sharp lift in selected bond yields, especially in
the US and France. Since the previous Market Outlook was
published, 10-year benchmarks have risen 27bps for US

Treasuries, to be well above the 5% threshold, and 40bps for
French bonds. The latter move contrasts with German bunds,
which have seen little change in yields at that tenor over the
month, though they are up more than 60bps year to date.
Several factors are behind the latest rise, with no single
cause dominating. For some of these factors, the underlying
conditions had been in evidence for some time, but recent
events focused attention on them. The rise has been primarily

a story of real yields: longer-run inflation expectations implied
by both breakevens and swaps have not increased that much.
For much of the year, fluctuations in oil prices – and thus the
near-term inflation outlook – fed into market views of central
bank policy, and longer yields as well. More recently, though,
this correlation has broken down. Shifts in expectations about
central bank policy have remained important for bond pricing,
as evident in the concentration of the increase in real yields.

The FOMC’s decision to increase the Fed funds rate, and flag in
the ‘dot plots’ that at least one more hike was likely, seems to
have been something of a trigger event for repricing, not only
for US yields. Also salient were the various actions taken by
the US Treasury to suppress long yields and improve the US
government’s debt-servicing position. These efforts had little
lasting effect and yields are now higher, and higher relative to
peers, than before. But they may have served to draw attention

to the growing issues around US fiscal sustainability. High
supply of debt from hyperscaler firms to fund the AI / data
centre boom has also likely pressured yields at the margin.
That said, fiscal concerns are unlikely to have been the main
issue driving the lift in US yields. The slope of the US yield
curve is noticeably flatter than at the beginning of the year;
in other words, short yields have risen even more than the
10-year yield, signifying tighter expected monetary policy

lifting yields across the curve. Another lens on this shift is that
2-year and other short-dated yields have increased more than
estimates of risk-neutral rate, such as that from the NY Fed.
Fiscal concerns have been more salient in the case of France,
where spreads to bunds have widened from around 70bps at
the beginning of the year to nearly 140bps currently, wider than
Greece or Italy, with about 40bps of the widening occurring in
the past month. Contagion to other euro area sovereigns has

been limited, with spreads widening around 22bps over the
month for Greece, Italy and Belgium, and 13bps for Spain.
Many factors lifting global bond yields ...
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FINANCIAL MARKETS
... and RBA policy rates outlook
In Australia, the RBA hiked the cash rate again at its late
September meeting, and a further hike at the November
meeting looks likely. This would take the cash rate to 4.85%, a
new cycle high.
While the forecasts the RBA published in August did not

support further rate hikes, in September these forecasts were
characterised as resting on several things going right and
certain risks not emerging. Some of these risks, including a re-
escalation of the war in the Middle East and thus energy prices,
have since crystallised. The post-meeting communication
and minutes also pointed to the July CPI result being above
expectations, as was June quarter GDP growth “at the margin”.
The RBA is also wary about pressure on both construction

costs and retail prices of IT goods arising from the AI/data
centre boom. No weight is put on the possibility that this new
investment and technology might add to productivity or the
capital stock, and so supply capacity, even with a lag. Indeed,
the post-meeting minutes highlighted the financial stability
risks if valuations in this area should disappoint “even if the
technology were ultimately successful”, as if the latter was not
yet clear. This language points to a generally downbeat view of

Australian supply capacity that also emerges in its discussion
of productivity trends. Although its position piece in the August
2025 Statement on Monetary Policy  indicated that lower trend
growth in productivity had no implications for inflation or
monetary policy, more recent statements imply that it does.
More broadly, the minutes for the September meeting revealed
a tendency to contemplate more hawkish interpretations
of data and outcomes. For example, the minutes dismissed

evidence of the labour market easing a bit more than
was forecast by pointing to the changes to the survey
arrangements. At the same time, the minutes contemplated
that the rate of unemployment consistent with inflation
being at target might have risen, even though wages growth
has marginally undershot the RBA’s forecasts and the cost
pressures cited by its business liaison contacts are mostly
non-labour costs. The minutes also included an extended

discussion of the possibility that the global neutral interest
rate might have increased recently. If true, this would imply
that financial conditions were less restrictive. This conclusion
would be hard to square with yield curves that have flattened
since the beginning of the year and other central bank
estimates of the term premium having increased, both of which
suggest tighter financial conditions.
That said, the RBA will find it hard to look past inflation

outcomes in the second half of year that is likely to remain
sticky. We anticipate trimmed mean inflation to record
quarterly outcomes of around 0.9%qtr in Q3 and Q4, remaining
around the current 3½%yr pulse. The Monetary Policy Board
will want to see clear evidence of inflation coming down before
withdrawing policy restrictiveness, so we continue to pencil in
August as the likely date for start of the rate-cutting phase.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUSTRALIAN ECONOMY
After posting a soft but better than expected first half, the
Australian economy is facing into more challenges near term
as another round of disruptions from global energy markets
combine with a further rise in interest rates and a deepening

housing downturn. Against this, business investment is in
the midst of a sustained ramp-up led by a large and growing
pipeline of data centre and energy investment projects.
Elsewhere, public demand is growing at a slower pace and
new dwelling investment is likely to moderate but both are
expected to still see positive growth. Overall, the demand
mix should see capacity pressures continue to ease but with
significant risks on either side.
Shifting political winds

Some of these risks are now in the wider policy space. The
political landscape has changed materially since the 2025
Federal Election with opinion polls showing a sustained swing
against the Labor government and a marked rise in support
for One Nation that raises the prospect of a three-horse race
in the next election, the outcome of which could be very hard
to predict (we may get a preview of this with next month’s
Victorian state election). While the next Federal election is

still a long way off – not due until well into 2028 – the shift
does appear to be having some bearing on policy. For example,
the government’s choice not to reinstate the temporary
halving in fuel excise tax that was deployed when fuel prices
first spiked back in March suggests it is less inclined towards
providing broad based cost-of-living support that might make
it harder to bring inflation back down.
Migration policy is shaping more clearly as a battleground.

A suite of changes announced by the government in mid-
September and which came into effect in October, are already
expected to slow working holidaymaker and foreign student
arrivals, moderating total net overseas migration from
around 300k current to 225k by 2028, trimming population
growth from around 1.4%yr to 1.2%yr. For its part, the main
Liberal-National opposition party has proposed to reduce net
migration to 100k in the first two years of government, before

increasing to 130k and 160k in the two years following –
which would likely see total population growth slow to around
0.7-0.8%yr initially. One Nation has proposed a period of ‘net
negative’ migration, lowering net migration by 250k a year
over the first three years of government before considering a
‘ceiling’ of 130k per year thereafter. This would see population
growth slow to around 0.2-0.3%yr initially.
While none of these policies are on the table for this year

or next, the direction of travel is clear with a risk that the
current government moves further towards curbing migrant
More challenges near term ...
Matthew Hassan, Head of Australian Macro-Forecasting
inflows. Slower population growth basically maps one-to-
one to GDP growth although the specific flow-on impacts to
areas like housing and labour markets are more complex and
will depend on the population mix and how the demand and
supply effects balance out. For the record, we have trimmed

our own population growth forecasts in the 2028 out-year,
from 1.4%yr to 1.2%yr with a related tweak to our GDP
growth forecast for that year.
Consumer sentiment-spending gap
Near term challenges continue to centre on the consumer.
Here, the latest updates show sentiment again plumbing
historical lows but more mixed messages around spending
which is still holding up better than might be expected based
on a simple read on the consumer mood.
Certainly that mood remains deeply negative. The Westpac–

Melbourne Institute Consumer Sentiment Index dropped
4.7% to 80.4 in October, putting the month amongst the forty
worst reads since the began in the early 1970s, following
two others reads in this group back in April and June and
another eight recorded in 2022–23. Altogether this marks the
worst period of recurring extremely weak sentiment since the
disastrous recession in the early 1990s.
This has been difficult to square with the soft rather than

outright weak picture on consumer spending through most
of this year. Even when real spending was recording more
material per capita declines back in 2023-24, these were
somewhat milder than what might have been expected given
the weakness in sentiment at the time.
We explored this more fully in our August Red Book . Some
of the disconnect may be a by-product of high inflation which
‘hits different’ sentiment-wise. Cost of living pressures,

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUSTRALIAN ECONOMY
... centred on uneven consumer, housing downturn ...
which are the central issue for consumers at the moment,
represent a broad-based but shallower squeeze rather than
the deeper shocks that typically come when big economic
downturns hit. Labour markets appear to be an important

point of distinction – despite the weakness in sentiment,
consumers have been relatively unperturbed about the jobs
outlook in recent years. Adding consumers’ unemployment
expectations to sentiment measures significantly improves its
‘fit’ as a predictor of per capita spending.
There other aspect that helps to reconcile the sentiment-
spending gap is the detailed picture across segments. There
may be one economy but there is not a single consumer.

Instead both sentiment and spending outcomes reflect the
average across a range of often quite varied conditions in
different consumer segments.
This shows through in the dispersion of sentiment across
sub-groups. The chart right shows the current distribution of
index reads across the 100-odd sub-groups we regularly track
with some selected groups called out. If the range seems wide
its because it is – measures of spread over the last two years
have been 50-60% wider than the average over the previous

three decades. Exactly why is not completely clear but the
observations point to a more fractured consumer with multiple
fault lines – including across states, metro/regional areas, voter
groups, labour force status, income levels and housing tenures.
Spending-wise, one of the clearest emerging fault lines is across
age groups. Here, there look to be three main ‘blocs’ – 18-34 year
olds (mainly millennials’); 35-54 year olds (mainly ‘gen X’); and

those aged over 55 (dominated by the ‘baby boomers’).
Demographically, the 55+ age group is both the largest and
the fastest-growing – our estimates suggest it now makes up
about 45% of all households, up 8.5pts compared to 2020. It
is also seeing significant financial shifts with the cohort coming
through carrying materially larger superannuation holdings
(reflecting a variety of factors including a longer period of
compulsory contributions that have earned compounding

returns) and more housing wealth. Again, our rough estimates
suggest average household wealth in this group is around
$2.5m up over $800k since 2020 (reflecting both the
aforementioned demographic shifts and valuation changes).
At the same time, the group has low debt levels – it is largely
untouched by interest rate increases and probably benefits
slightly insofar as this lifts interest income both directly and
indirectly via super holdings.
For 18-34 year olds, incomes are more important than balance

sheets, meaning inflation poses more of a constraint. For 35-
54 year olds, income is less of a constraint but high debt levels
poses a big challenge as rate rises increase servicing costs.
All of which is to say that it should come as little surprise that
spending by the 55+ age group has been running stronger
than the other age groups. Breakdowns from our Westpac
Consumer Panel show average non fuel spend has been rising
at a 5% annual pace over the last six months amongst those

AUSTRALIAN ECONOMY
aged 55+. That compares to a more sedate 2.7-2.9% pace 
across the other age groups, which given the current pace of 
inflation, implies small declines in real, inflation-adjusted terms.
These demographic contrasts also account for the observed 
strength in key bellwether spending categories like new 
vehicle purchases. The total number of new car purchases 
by consumers is up close to 4%yr, led by strong growth in 
EVs. Notably, industry figures suggest the purchase of used 
cars is down sharply, by close to 9%yr (used car sales are 
not well covered in the statistics and largely bypass official 
ABS measures because the are mainly transfers between 
households rather than a new addition). 
Pulling these clues together, at least some of the resilience in 
spending looks to be a form of ‘wealth effect’ where balance 
sheet considerations are more important than the real purchasing 
power of incomes. While the impact of employment, wages, 
inflation and interest rate changes will still likely be the dominant 
driver of shifts in spending growth, the nature of this wealth 
effect and how it evolves from here could be a key swing factor. 
Housing downturn deepens
And where that swing factor may start to turn is in response 
to a deepening downturn in Australia’s housing market. The 
combined impact of interest rate rises and major changes to 
housing-related tax policy announced in the Federal budget is 
continuing to weigh heavily. We have been flagging for some 
time that uncertainty could see a more significant ‘air pocket’ 
emerge with very thin trading conditions and a sharper move 
lower in prices. That risk is crystallising with turnover already 
down 20% and prices off 8%. 
With the September interest rate rise and an expected hike 
in November still to come, more weakness is likely near term. 
We revised our house price forecasts lower with a decline of 
around 8% now expected for calendar 2026, with a peak-to-
trough decline of just over 10%. 
... wealth effects an uncertain swing factor 
This constitutes a more material housing downturn. The 
peak to trough fall is set to be biggest since the beginning of 
the national price measures in 1980, although prices in real, 
inflation-adjusted terms recorded larger falls during the early-
1990s and early-1980s recessions with capital city measures 
also suggesting there were larger real price falls during the 
recessions in the mid-1970s and early 1960s. 
It also points to a bigger housing-related drag on growth. 
As discussed previously, the main channels are through 
new dwelling investment, turnover and wealth effects. New 
dwelling investment is still carrying solid momentum (the 
number of dwelling under construction up 10% over the year 
to June) but is expected to see a slightly more pronounced 
slowing. Turnover is also expected to be slightly weaker. 
However, it is the wealth effects from price declines that is the 
area to watch. Our rough estimates suggest this will account 
for most of the –0.8ppts on annual GDP growth over the 
year to June 2027. The emphasis here though is on ‘rough’. 
Wealth effects have seeming disappeared for long periods 
and their precise timing is very unclear, e.g. how should the 
drag from the current downturn be viewed against what 
were presumably strong positives coming off the much larger 
preceding gains? Some of this could be due to ‘assymmetric’ 
behaviour, with effects playing out very differently when there 
wealth is rising or falling.
On balance, we expect the impact to see consumer spending 
‘muddle through’ rather than fall back to the weaker growth 
seen during the more intense cost of living and interest 
rate shocks in 2023-24. This partly reflects the indirect 
support coming from growth more generally – the AI-related 
investment boom, which we have covered at length in previous 
reports, a key factor helping to hold GDP growth up around 
1.5%yr and prevent a more material weakening in labour 
market conditions. For the consumer though this will continue 
to feel like a struggle with growth flat in per capita terms 
and a shakier picture around housing that will play out very 
differently for some segments.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
MIDDLE EAST
Low inventories and higher shipping costs will keep oil and
fuel prices elevated, with Brent futures forecast to average
US$101/bbl in Q4 before easing to US$77/bbl by Q4 2027
as supply recovers. Refined fuels and LNG prices should
ease more slowly. Risks are two-sided: escalation could

push Brent towards US$150/bbl, while an early US-Iran
agreement could see a faster normalisation in supply with
prices returning close to pre-conflict levels by end-2027.
Brent crude is back above US$100/bbl on fading confidence
in a lasting US-Iran agreement and intermittent attacks on
shipping. Buffers remain depleted, with the IEA estimating
global observed inventories were 507mb lower at the end of
August than at the start of the conflict.
This comes despite crude exports through the Strait of Hormuz

recovering to around 70% to 90% of pre-conflict levels in late
September. Saudi and UAE exports through the Yanbu and
Fujairah pipelines continue, with the East-West pipeline back
to normal after the early September attacks. Tanker crossings
have risen, although transponders remain switched off, leaving
visible transit at 1% to 4% of pre-conflict levels, while cargo
transfers in the Gulf of Oman have increased.
However, these workarounds are slower and more costly.

Ship-to-ship transfers can take 5 to 10 days, US escorts cause
delays and some tankers are taking longer routes. Fewer ships
are also available, with many redeployed to other markets.
Higher insurance premiums are adding to costs, with Gulf-to-
China tanker rates above US$0.9mn a day, around 15x higher
than a year ago. With prompt barrels scarce, the gap between
physical and futures prices widened again, with Dated Brent
around US$136/bbl in early October against US$101/bbl for

front-month futures.
Oil and fuel prices push higher ...
Sian Fenner, Head of Business & Industry Economics
Justin Smirk, Senior Economist
Luka Belobajdic, Economist
Refined prices have risen more than crude
Refined product markets were already tight going into the
conflict. Reduced Gulf supply, exacerbated by Ukrainian strikes
on Russian refineries, has added to that tightness. Together
with higher shipping costs and rising Dated Brent prices,
Singapore Gasoil 10ppm (diesel) has roughly doubled since the

Strait closed, compared with around 40% for crude futures.
Consequently, Singapore gasoil crack spread widened to
an average of around US$36.1/bbl in early October, from
US$17.5/bbl across 2025. Alongside the government’s excise
tax cut returning to normal, this has lifted national diesel and
petrol prices to around A$2.83/L and A$2.41/L.
Outlook
We do not see a lasting US-Iran agreement until at least
mid-2027. In the meantime, we assume there will not be a

return to full-scale military intervention. However, periodic
Iranian attacks on ships and facilities are expected to continue,
as will the US blockade of Iranian exports, keeping a supply
risk premium in place. We look for Brent futures to average
around US$101/bbl in Q4, then ease gradually as demand
from refiners and governments to rebuild stocks partly offsets
recovering supply. As the premium fades and the market
moves into surplus in late 2027, we forecast Brent at around

US$77/bbl in Q4 2027, easing towards US$70/bbl in 2028 as
Middle East production fully recovers.
Oil balance to gradually improve over 2027
Global oil supply is expected to rise by 4.0mb/d (4.0%) to
103.7mb/d in 2027, following a 6.1% fall in 2026. Most of this
reflects a gradual recovery in Gulf output, with Houthi attacks
on Red Sea shipping keeping Saudi production below its

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
MIDDLE EAST
... as supply still tight ...
With product supply recovering slowly and depleted inventories
being rebuilt, Singapore gasoil crack is likely to stay above its
long-run average even as Brent eases, narrowing from US$71/
bbl in Q4 to US$27/bbl by Q4 2027. With Singapore Gas

10ppm prices, shipping and insurance costs to remain elevated
and easing only gradually in 2027, we forecast Australian diesel
to retail at an average of 274¢/L in Q4, easing to 200¢/L in Q4
2027, and petrol at 230¢/L and 187¢/L respectively.
LNG prices supported through 2027
Japanese LNG prices surged 15.2% in September, averaging
US$25.4/mmbtu as supply disruptions in Qatar and the Strait
of Hormuz tightened markets and intensified competition.

Our Q4 forecast for Japanese LNG is now US$24.5/mmbtu
and we expect the market to remain structurally tight, with
supply recovery likely to be gradual and prices not returning
to pre-conflict levels until 2028. Still, Australia’s domestic
gas market should remain insulated due to the current gas
reservation arrangements.
Risks around our oil price outlook are two-sided
Talks are still ongoing in the background, with the US and
Iran exchanging proposals to reopen the Strait, but the gap

between them remains wide. The conflict has also expanded
to the Red Sea and, with inventories sharply drawn down,
there is a smaller buffer to absorb a further shock.
Using the Oxford Economics model, we assess an escalation
scenario, where energy markets face a deeper and more
persistent disruption as the conflict in the Middle East
intensifies. In this case, Brent futures rise to around US$150/
bbl in H1 2027, only dipping to below US$100/bbl in Q2 2028.

We also consider an early resolution scenario, where shipping
and Gulf supply normalise faster, returning Brent to near pre-
conflict levels by end-2027.
Escalation scenario
In the escalation scenario we assume:
Talks stall and the conflict escalates:  the gap between the
US and Iran proves too wide and the US resumes strikes after
the midterms. Iran responds with attacks on shipping and Gulf
energy infrastructure, damaging export, storage and refining

facilities. A lasting agreement is not reached until 2028.
Export routes are disrupted: more frequent attacks curtail
dark crossings of the Strait and ship-to-ship transfers in the
Gulf of Oman. Houthi attacks also cut off Saudi alternative
routes, forcing Gulf producers to cut output and Middle
East crude exports sharply lower. New routes and shipping
arrangements eventually support a recovery in Gulf flows, but
trade patterns take time to adjust.

Product supply fails to recover:  The Russia–Ukraine energy
ceasefire does not hold and strikes on Russian refineries
pre-conflict level until H2 2027. Meanwhile, we look for UAE
production to continue to improve over 2027 and lift above
pre-conflict levels from 2028, as it is no longer constrained by
OPEC quotas. We do not expect a meaningful return of Iranian
oil until an agreement is reached, and damage sustained
during the conflict means production will take time to recover.

We expect global oil demand to be 103.7mb/d in 2026, 0.8mb/d
(0.7%) higher than in 2025, and to rise a further 1.9mb/d (1.8%)
to 105.6mb/d in 2027. Chinese crude imports will continue to
recover over the coming months as refiners restock ahead of
Lunar New Year, but we expect only a gradual return to pre-
conflict levels, as the electrification of transport limits the
recovery in Chinese demand. Globally, demand for diesel and jet
fuel recovers as transport and aviation activity picks up, although

some demand destruction and efficiency gains are expected
to be permanent. The conflict has also highlighted strategic
vulnerabilities, particularly across Asia, so we expect a push to
rebuild reserves adding around 0.5% to demand over 2027.
Fuel prices to ease more slowly than crude
Refined product supply is expected to recover more slowly
than crude, with Gulf refinery runs not returning to pre-war
levels until at least late 2027. Outside the Gulf, there has

been some constructive news. The US has lifted sanctions
on Russian diesel until April 2027, with Russia saying it will
supply more than 4Mt over the coming months, while China
has lifted its ban on refined product exports. However, we
are cautious about how much this will add to the market in
the short term. Much of the Russian supply is headed to the
US and will depend on Russia’s ability to restore refining and
export capacity, while doubts remain over the Russia-Ukraine

energy ceasefire announced by President Trump. China still
manages exports through quotas, which are likely to increase
only gradually as domestic stocks are rebuilt. Refinery runs
should lift ahead of Lunar New Year, but any increase in
exports will take time to reach regional markets.

Demand settles below pre-conflict trend: Some demand
destruction and efficiency gains persist, particularly in China.
With inventories rebuilding faster, government restocking is
smaller and more gradual than in the baseline.

Prices fall quickly, but restocking provides a floor:  Brent
Brent futures ease to around US$88/bbl in Q1 2027 as the
premium for prompt physical barrels unwinds. As the market
moves into balance, Brent eases to around US$65/bbl by Q4
2027, close to pre-conflict levels.
Refined product prices ease faster on lower freight costs with
the Singapore gasoil crack narrowing. Australian diesel and
petrol prices fall to around 182 cpl and 170 cpl by Q4 2027.

Gas markets also ease, although damage to Qatar’s gas facility
means export capacity recovers gradually. Japanese LNG falls to
around US$11.9/MMBtu by end-2027, $2.1 below our baseline.
Economic impact
In the escalation scenario, higher fuel costs flow through to
transport, food and other energy-intensive goods such as
plastics and fertilisers. Australian headline CPI remains above
4.5% for much of 2027, with underlying inflation remaining
sticky at above 3% until late 2028. The RBA faces a difficult

trade off between much higher inflation and a significant
hit to activity, as households cut discretionary spending and
businesses defer investment. Higher LNG export earnings
provide only a moderate offset. In the early resolution
scenario, headline inflation is around 0.1ppt below the
baseline in 2027.
New Zealand is more exposed in both scenarios, with food
and energy a larger share of its CPI and no offsetting energy
exports. Headline inflation is around 1ppt higher in the

escalation scenario in Q1 2027.
continue, limiting how much diesel Russia can export,
even with the US sanctions waiver extended. China also
reintroduces its export ban to protect domestic supply.
Oil prices lift to around US$150/bbl in H1 2027: With little
buffer to absorb the supply loss, competition for physical
delivery intensifies, with physical prices well above futures
as prompt barrels remain scarce. Higher prices support
supply growth outside OPEC and drive demand destruction,

alongside further emergency stock releases, but a deeper
and more prolonged global deficit remains. Brent stays above
US$100/bbl until mid-2028 as governments rebuild reserves.
The Singapore gasoil crack widens and, with freight and
insurance costs also higher, Australian diesel and petrol prices
average around 247 cpl and 231 cpl at end 2027. Japanese
LNG also remains elevated around US$23.2/MMBtu in Q4
2027, $9.1 above our baseline.
Early resolution scenario

In the early resolution scenario we assume:
A durable agreement: The US and Iran agree terms to reopen
the Strait by the end of year, and, unlike the June interim deal,
the agreement holds, with progress towards a permanent
settlement in early 2027. Attacks on shipping cease and the
US blockade is lifted, allowing Iranian oil back into the market,
initially from storage. Vessels are quickly repositioned to the
Gulf, with freight and war-risk insurance easing from early

2027 rather than mid-year.
A faster recovery in supply:  With export routes secure, Gulf
production and damaged refinery and export capacity recover
sooner than in the baseline. Outside the Gulf, the US extends
its waiver on Russian diesel beyond April 2027 and China
raises export quotas as domestic stocks are rebuilt.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Most commodities rose in September, with many of the
themes that drove moves across August remaining in place.
Our broadest commodities index increased 5.6%mth,
led by coking coal, which rose 22.3% on ongoing supply
tightness following the Shanxi mine explosion. Japanese

LNG (+15.2%mth) and Brent oil (+14.9%mth) also recorded
strong gains amid persistent Middle East tensions, while
thermal coal rose 11.7%mth. Copper (+1.3%mth) and
aluminium (+1.0%mth) extended their recent gains, while
iron ore managed a meagre advance (+0.7%mth), supported
by higher freight and production costs. Gold fell 2.1%mth
following the Fed’s September rate hike, while lithium
was the weakest performer, with spodumene prices down
4.8%mth amid renewed oversupply concerns.

India increasingly the swing factor for coal
Newcastle thermal coal prices rose 11.7%mth in September,
averaging US$146/t over the month. LNG markets continue
to support the relative cost competitiveness of thermal coal,
while China has also re-emerged as a stronger seaborne
buyer, reflecting tighter domestic supply following the Shanxi
mining disaster. El Niño has contributed to hotter conditions
and a weaker monsoon season, boosting electricity demand

while weighing heavily on hydro generation. Against this
backdrop, we now expect Newcastle thermal coal to average
US$130/t in the December quarter before easing towards
US$116/t through 2027 as global energy prices recede.
Queensland premium low-vol coking coal prices led gains
across the commodities complex in September, rising
22.3%mth to average US$275.8/t. The increase reflects
strong Chinese demand, with buying accelerating ahead
of the peak steelmaking season, compounded by domestic

supply shortages following the Shanxi mining disaster. The
resulting supply loss was initially expected to be offset by
increased Mongolian imports. However, diesel shortages
have constrained local coal production. Looking ahead, the
normalisation of Mongolian exports and weaker Chinese
steelmaker margins are expected to limit demand for seaborne
cargoes, although India should provide some offset as the
country emerges from its monsoon season and steel production

lifts. As a result, we forecast premium low-vol coking coal
prices to remain elevated in the December quarter, averaging
US$250/t before moderating through 2027.
Cost pressures place a floor under iron ore prices
Iron ore prices (62% Fe index) rose 0.7%mth in September
to average US$98.3/t, largely reflecting ongoing pressure
from elevated freight and energy costs. Shipping futures
from Western Australia to Qingdao continued to rise with
the September average sitting 91% above the 2025 average.

Recent trends remain firmly in place ...

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
... with energy markets leading the gains
COMMODITIES
Evidence of cost pressures is becoming increasingly apparent
with Wood Mackenzie estimating the 90th percentile cost of
production now sits at US$79.8/t -- 5.7% above 2025 levels.
That said, major headwinds facing iron ore remain firmly in place.

Seaborne supply continues to expand while at the same time, the
Shanxi mining disaster has kept coking coal markets particularly
tight, further compressing steelmaker margins. China’s domestic
economy also presents limited upside, with urban fixed asset
investment down 7.2%ytd in August and iron ore inventories at
Chinese ports remaining historically elevated. We expect the
62% Fe index to average US$95/t in the December quarter.
The medium-term outlook is becoming increasingly challenging.

Supply-side pressures are building as new low-cost output
from Simandou enters the market. Persistently high inventories
in China and softer global steel demand, as major economies
contend with elevated energy costs, are expected to place
further downward pressure on prices. The ongoing structural
decline in Chinese steel production is also eroding underlying
demand. We therefore expect iron ore prices to soften further,
averaging around US$83/t in the December quarter of 2027 as

surplus conditions become more evident.
Gulf disruptions continue to support aluminium
Aluminium prices rose 1.0%mth in September, averaging
US$3,284.2/t. Continued disruption to Gulf smelting capacity
has maintained upward pressure on the market, with the Gulf
region accounting for around 9% of global smelting capacity and
a larger share of global trade. While limited volumes of metal are
beginning to emerge from the Middle East, difficulties securing

sufficient alumina feedstock continue to constrain production.
As a result, IAI statistics indicate GCC production in August
remained 42.6%yr below August 2025 levels. Given the time-
and capital-intensive nature of restarting Gulf smelting capacity,
we expect market tightness to persist through the remainder of
2026. Additional near-term support is likely to come from China
where aluminium output increased by just 3.1%yr in August,
offsetting only around half of lost GCC production, with growth

constrained by China’s capacity replacement policy.
Against this backdrop, we expect aluminium prices to maintain
strength across the remainder of the year. However, we do not
anticipate a full return of the war premium, with higher prices
incentivising capacity restarts across Western economies and
South-East Asia, while Chinese demand remains subdued. As a
result, we forecast prices to average US$3,250/t in the December
quarter. Thereafter, prices are expected to ease as supply

stabilises, Gulf producers restore production, and new Indonesian
smelting capacity backed by Chinese investment comes online.
Chinese policy fuels lithium oversupply fears
Markets were spooked by renewed concerns around lithium
oversupply in September after China froze approvals for
greenfield power and battery energy storage system factories
that had yet to break ground. The move reflects growing concerns
around excess capacity in the sector and forms part of Beijing’s

broader anti-involution agenda. Sentiment was further subdued
by increased focus on the potential restart of the Jianxiawo
mine, which accounts for around 3-4% of global lithium supply
at full capacity and remains suspended pending environmental
approvals. Subsequently, Spodumene (6% FOB Australia) prices
fell 4.8%mth in September to average US$2,007/t.
Despite this, the near-term outlook remains constructive.
Demand should remain supported by lean downstream

inventories and continued investment in energy storage
solutions following the recent energy shock. Additional
growth is likely to come from accelerating EV adoption, with
lower-cost Chinese EVs continuing to gain market share. Over
the medium to longer term, additional supply, particularly from
Australia, is expected to weigh on prices. Further, recent tax
changes in China, which impose a consumption tax on lithium-
ion batteries while exempting sodium-ion and solid-state

batteries until end-2028, may encourage some substitution
away from lithium at the margin. Nevertheless, lithium is
expected to remain the dominant battery chemistry.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
After struggling to break up through 100 over the past
four months, in late September and early-October the DXY
index popped higher to near 103, a high back to early-2025.
Underlying the move are expectations that inflation will
prove persistent globally, requiring an extended period

of higher rates as well as a belief that the US tech sector
leaves it best positioned to weather the storm.
It is important to note at the top that available data does not
support a view that the US/Rest-of-World divide has grown.
US GDP growth certainly remains above average and the
Europe/UK below, but this has been the case throughout 2026.
Moreover, Europe and UK have shown greater-than-
anticipated resilience of late, and growth in both jurisdictions

is expected to strengthen as the US slows into 2027. Also
relevant for DXY, Canada looks to be on a similar path to
Europe, despite the ill effects of US trade policy.
Participants also expect a run of rate hikes in each jurisdiction
through the remainder of 2026 and in 2027. We believe these
expectations are unlikely to come to pass, though our cross-
market view is proportion to the market’s – i.e. we see each
central bank biding their time after a final hike in coming

months, allowing the currently restrictive stance of policy to
take effect over time.
At the long end of the curve meanwhile, yields are certainly in
the US dollar’s favour. While this is in part due to the strong
expectations for US growth, it also has to do with growing
anxiety over the US’ fiscal position, unpredictable policy and
key global investors reconsidering the scale of their exposure
to US government debt.
None of these latter factors are a positive for the US dollar

today, and the risk is the US becomes more of an outlier on
each front as greater co-ordination delivers a windfall for
sentiment and growth across Europe and Canada.
As such, we continue to believe the US dollar will broadly
move lower over the coming 24 months, though it could take
until early-2027 for the turn to become definitively evident.
From EUR/USD1.12 today, we forecast the euro to rise to
USD1.14 at year end, then USD1.21 in December 2027 and

USD1.22 in June 2028.
Sterling is expected to follow, from USD1.32 today to
USD1.34 at end-2026, then USD1.39 by end-2027 and
USD1.41 come June 2028.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
The Canadian dollar is also anticipated to appreciate over
the forecast period, USD/CAD falling from CAD1.42 today to
CAD1.34 by end-2027 and CAD1.32 mid-2028.
Along with sustained modest gains for yen (see below), this
should see the DXY index at 95.1 by end-2027, then 94.5

around the middle of 2028.
Turning to Asia, the yen remains in focus. Growth in Japan
is solid and inflation around target, warranting a continued
gradual normalisation of the policy rate. It is clear though
that the Bank of Japan is not progressing at the pace
market participants desire, and so pressure remains on the
currency. As we have often highlighted, returns from trade
and investment also continue to hold the yen back, despite
improving profitability for Japanese listed companies and a

broadly stable rate differential.
We expect these negative factors to abate and risks against
yen to dissipate, but only slowly. Consequently, we only
forecast USD/JPY to fall from JPY158 currently to around
JPY146 come end-2028. Into the medium-term, we expect
to see further gains, at some stage at a much more rapid
pace. Note, our end-2028 forecast of JPY146 is 32% above
the average of the decade prior to the pandemic, providing
significant scope for rapid yen gains if sentiment improves.

On to China, where investors are increasingly anticipating
additional stimulus and also recognising the global
opportunity before Chinese industry. As detailed on page
18, recent efforts to support the economy have been highly
targeted, though they point to a broader push by authorities
coming in late-2026 and early next year. The current initiatives
also give support to the idea that the Central Government
intends to increasingly allow the private sector and effective

state-linked entities to deploy capital and scale on their own
terms, albeit still within the country’s long-term master plan
centred on technology and other high value-add industry.
For China, the returns from trade have already been extensive,
but there is scope for decades more to come. Still, the coming
years are more likely to see development in sync with local
neighbours such as Vietnam, Korea and Indonesia, mitigating
large-scale bilateral appreciation within the region. Instead,

it is currency trading against the US dollar that will highlight
performance and expectations for the future. While we expect
most of the region’s currencies to appreciate materially against
the US dollar through end-2028, the timing is likely to differ.
Singapore’s dollar has already run up, limiting further gains.
China’s renminbi is likely next in line to appreciate materially
against the US dollar, followed by the won and Taiwan’s dollar.
India’s rupee and Indonesia’ rupiah are examples of currencies

within the region likely to gain with a delay, particularly while
energy prices remain elevated.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Since the September Market Outlook, we have seen significant
increases in global energy prices, a further fall in the New
Zealand dollar and increases in long term interest rates. These
developments have prompted us revise our macroeconomic
outlook for 2026 and 2027 which now incorporate a less

favourable mix of higher inflation and weaker growth. We
continue to expect the economy to grow above trend, although
with higher-for-longer inflation that justifies higher policy
rates from the RBNZ. The New Zealand dollar seems weak
and downside risks are growing should the economy falter
or if the USD continues to shift higher. The General Election
is on 7 November, and polls suggest that this remains a tight
race. Whichever bloc wins, we expect complex coalition

negotiations that may take some time to resolve.
Data on economic activity confirmed that the economy
continued to grow through the Iran-war-impacted June
quarter, registering the expected 0.2% growth rate. This was
a pleasing result considering our earlier concerns that the
economy might have gone backwards as energy prices and
economic uncertainty spiked. In addition, historical revisions to
GDP showed stronger growth in the pre-war quarters so that

annual growth was notably stronger than thought at 2.6%yr.
This means that the NZ economy has been running ahead of
its potential growth rate – estimated at around 1.6%yr by
the RBNZ for the year to June 2026. Concerns of a widening
output gap through 2026 appear to have been unfounded.
That is not to say that the economy is in rude health. The general
perception of economic conditions by consumers and businesses
is that times are tough. The dissonance between the data

and the perception reflects the still narrow nature of drivers
propelling the economy forward. Unusually, it is not the housing
market and related sectors such as household consumption that
is generating much of the growth. Rather, it is the agricultural
sector, exporters and tourism that are experiencing strong
conditions. This explains the wide dispersion of economic
indicators around the economy as evidenced by much stronger
conditions in the North vs the South Islands. We continue to see

strong export prices and have revised up our forecast milk price
for the 2026/27 season to $9.75/kg/ms and made an opening
estimate of the 2027/28 milk price of $9.50/kg/ms. These are
strong returns that will underpin demand and investment in the
agricultural sector in the years ahead.
We are seeing signs that lower interest rates are supporting
growth in interest sensitive sectors. For example, in the
June quarter the construction sector delivered its strongest

contribution to growth since 2023. We are also seeing signs
of strengthening employment growth including in the North
Island where urban areas predominate. However, the overall
Growth continued through the Middle East conflict ...
mix of growth remains relatively narrow which means many
sectors – especially the services sector – are missing out on
growth at this stage of the economic recovery.
The narrow nature of the recovery means that the economy

remains vulnerable to shocks and setbacks. The last month
or so has seen several negative shocks that are challenging
the recovery. A couple seem especially relevant: renewed
hostilities in the Middle East and a rise in petrol prices to new
highs; and a rise in global long term interest rates that are
adding to the tightening in financial conditions caused by the
RBNZ’s OCR increases since July 2026.
Thus far, businesses and households are showing much better

resilience to higher energy costs than shown early in the
conflict when petrol prices were close to current levels. We
have seen some decline in business and consumer confidence,
but the declines appear modest and remain consistent with
ongoing economic growth in the second half of 2026. The
question is how long that resilience will last?
We have reviewed our economic forecasts to account for
changes seen since late August when we last reviewed
our forecasts. The biggest changes concern the short-term

inflation outlook where higher fuel prices should flow through
to the CPI in the December quarter, adding more than 0.5% to
our prior estimate of inflation for the 2026 calendar year. That
means headline CPI inflation will end 2026 over 4% which is
an uncomfortable – but unavoidable – prospect for the RBNZ.
The weaker exchange rate will also add to inflation through
mid-2027 with the TWI down 4-5% since late August.
A key question is what impact will stronger near-term price

pressures have on the medium-term inflation outlook, which the
RBNZ will be most focused on? Business and consumer surveys
released in the last month showed no sign of rising inflation
expectations or increased business pricing pressures. This
suggests that most players in the economy see the energy shock
as a short-term disruption that will not persistently lift inflation.
At this juncture it certainly seems likely that headline inflation
will remain either above or in the top part of the RBNZ’s 1-3%

target range through most of 2027. But we have little evidence
to date that core inflation pressures will be similarly lifted. We
will review the Q3 CPI data next week for signs of broader
passthrough. In the June quarter we were pleasantly surprised
that core inflation measures remained contained.
It may well be that the still significant level of excess capacity
in the economy and the narrowness of growth can conspire
to keep broader inflation pressures under control. We have

trimmed our growth forecasts over the Q4 2026-Q2 2027
NEW ZEALAND
Kelly Eckhold
Chief Economist NZ

period to reflect these economic headwinds. We continue to
see solid growth that remains above the still poor potential
growth rate of the economy. But it is certainly the case that
absent other positive drivers, growth will be weaker than
previously thought. This should help constrain inflation

pressures all else equal.
Another area of the economy that has continued to surprise to
the downside is the housing market which has not met even
our modest expectations for a stabilisation in prices through
the second half of 2026. Unusually, despite low interest rates
house prices are if anything falling more quickly as the year
goes on. House sales are weakening and the time to sell a
house is lengthening.  We have revised down our house price

expectations for 2026 and 2027 to a fall of 1.4%yr in 2026
and a rise of just 1.75% in 2027. Weaker housing market
conditions likely mean consumption and residential investment
will be constrained leaving more of the burden for growth on
the external sector.
All this leaves us wondering if the RBNZ has enough to go on to
shift its strategy communicated in September. Given perceived
downside risks to activity, despite the uncomfortable near-term
inflation outlook, it indicated a preference to slow the pace of

OCR increases by pausing at its October meeting before raising
the OCR to 3% in December. Our sense is that the strategy will
remain in place unless the CPI data next week shows signs of
building core inflation pressures.
Markets have priced in a relatively high chance of hikes in both
October and December (around 33bps of hikes across the
two meetings). At times, pricing appears to have been highly
correlated with similar shifts in foreign markets as global oil

prices increased over September (although pricing has been
relatively stable of late). There will no doubt be a vigorous
debate within the MPC over how to balance the opposing
risks to inflation and growth. We suspect they will hold the

The exchange rate has been particularly weak in the last

easily weaken further as investors vote with their feet.
Another issue is the forthcoming 7 November election and the
associated uncertainty given that polling is tight and policy
divergences significant, especially between smaller parties
who will need to be coaxed into a coalition with one of the

larger parties. Fiscal risks already seem prominent in the fiscal
plans of both the National and Labour party. Deals done to
create a coalition likely increase those risks with the potential
to undermine the NZ dollar further post-election. It certainly
seems the case that markets have a strong focus on fiscal
sustainability right now.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
CHINA
There has been little change in China’s key narratives
year-to-date. The trade surplus remains near record highs,
buoyed by demand for green technology. Offsetting
external strength, however, has been a moribund consumer,
deteriorating business investment and continued month-on-

month declines for housing activity.
With each month that passes, the risk of disappointing
authorities’ 4.5-5.0% ambition grows. In our view, while the
Government is right to take a targeted approach to support for
the economy, it must quickly multiply the scale and breadth of
support if downside risks for 2027 and 2028 are to be kept at bay.
September’s endeavour with housing is a case in point. Late in
the month, authorities announced mortgage subsidies. Under

the policy, first-home buyers are to receive an annualised 1
percentage point reduction in the loan principal for up to 5
years. However, the policy only applies to homes of up to 120
square metres with a maximum value of 1.5mn yuan.
Industry data from Proptech Innovations reported by
Bloomberg suggest new home sales were up 15% from a year
ago during October’s National Day celebrations, but sales were
still 9% below the same period in 2024.
After more than 4 years of declining prices, sales and new

construction, targeting this small sub-set of buyers is best
interpreted as a first step towards broader support, allowing
young buyers to get in ahead of the pack.
A similar scenario has been playing out in China’s equity
market, with several rounds of buying by state-linked
institutional investors building a floor for Chinese shares this
year while changes to these investors’ long-term investment
mandates lock in sustained buying over the years ahead.

Hot IPOs have also been allowed to run, arguably in part to
highlight the opportunity China’s tech industry has before it to
scale and, in time, deliver significant wealth gains.
Suggesting further support is coming, also included in the
late-September stimulus initiative was support for targeted
lending by the banks for technology innovation and equipment
upgrades, along with infrastructure finance. While open to
all businesses, these initiatives seek to protect supply for

small companies to avoid past issues with credit rationing.
Increasingly then, Chinese businesses of all sizes are positioned
to take advantage of growth opportunities.
In the very near term, the scaling of these private enterprises
is likely to prove a support for sentiment and market valuations
more so than employment. To quickly boost household

development offers Chinese public and private issuers an
opportunity to lock in long-term capital at highly attractive
rates. At the current juncture, this is a significant advantage
to the West who are facing heightened inflations risks and
fiscal uncertainty. While many harbour concerns on the latter
point for China too, the control Chinese authorities have
over outbound foreign investment means a greater share of

domestic capital is captive to local opportunities than is the
case across open developed markets.
All told, the hold-up for a broadening of growth across China’s
economy is therefore confidence. When this occurs, however,
is up to the Central Government.
